In elementary school I was always the kid who couldn’t stay in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher, blurt stuff out, or get up and wander around the room, and I got notes sent home for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your impulsive and inattentive behaviors are causing problems for you in your daily life beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s not just work in the moment, it kind of follows me. I’ve been written up for safety and production issues, and now I’m worried it could mess up my job and the certification stuff I’m trying to do. And coworkers don’t really want to partner with me during handoffs as much, so it makes the day harder and kind of embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these impulsive and inattentive behaviors consistently throughout your life, without any significant periods where they weren't a problem?\n\nPatient: It’s been there in some way for a long time, but it’s been causing serious problems for about a year and two months now. That’s around when I started trying to tighten up my habits and move toward getting forklift and CNC certified. Since then it’s been harder to brush off, because it’s actually getting in the way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep leaving my station at work when I’m supposed to stay there. Like, I’ll just pop up during meetings or take off from my spot before I even really think about it. My supervisor said it’s starting to be a safety problem, so… that’s what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're acting on impulse when you leave your station at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels impulsive. I don’t really plan to leave—I just kind of pop up or take off from my station before I even think it through. Then afterward I realize I was supposed to stay put, and my supervisor’s said it’s becoming a safety issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. If I’m in a safety meeting or one of those certification classes, my foot is bouncing the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen, picking at my sleeves, shifting around in the chair. People will actually ask me to stop because I’m moving so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself acting on impulse in situations outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll cut in on people without really meaning to. Like if coworkers are talking, I’ll jump into the conversation, or if the lead is asking something, I’ll answer before they’re even done talking. Then people get irritated with me, and I realize I should’ve waited.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to what you're doing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like with inventory logs or reading the CNC training stuff, I’ll start off trying to focus, but after a few minutes the numbers or words kind of run together. Then I realize I didn’t really take it in, so I have to keep going back and rereading it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your impulsive behaviors are affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I cut people off without meaning to, like I’ll jump into a conversation or answer before someone’s even finished asking. I’d talk over the teacher, blurt stuff out, or get up and wander around the room, and I got notes sent home for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your impulsive and inattentive behaviors are causing problems for you in your daily life beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s not just work in the moment, it kind of follows me. I’ve been written up for safety and production issues, and now I’m worried it could mess up my job and the certification stuff I’m trying to do. And coworkers don’t really want to partner with me during handoffs as much, so it makes the day harder and kind of embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these impulsive and inattentive behaviors consistently throughout your life, without any significant periods where they weren't a problem?\n\nPatient: It’s been there in some way for a long time, but it’s been causing serious problems for about a year and two months now. I’ve been written up for safety and production problems, and it feels like it could put my job and the certifications I’m trying to get at risk. Even coworkers don’t really want to partner with me during handoffs as much now, so it’s stressful and embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your problems with paying attention or your impulsive behaviors cause you more trouble in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I think the impulsive part causes me more trouble. I just kind of take off before I even think about it, and my supervisor is saying it’s becoming a safety problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your impulsive behaviors or trouble paying attention are causing you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does. I’ve already been written up for safety and production stuff, and now some coworkers don’t really want to work with me during handoffs. It makes me feel embarrassed and worried, especially with the certifications I’m trying to get.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention or with being too active and impulsive?\n\nPatient: More with being too active and impulsive, I think. I keep leaving my station or getting up in meetings when I’m supposed to stay put, and it’s like I do it before I even think. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep leaving my station at work when I’m supposed to stay there. Like, I’ll just pop up during meetings or take off from my spot before I even really think about it. My supervisor said it’s starting to be a safety problem, so… that’s what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're acting on impulse when you leave your station at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels impulsive. I don’t really plan to leave—I just kind of pop up or take off from my station before I even think it through. Then afterward I realize I was supposed to stay put, and my supervisor’s said it’s becoming a safety issue.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. If I’m in a safety meeting or one of those certification classes, my foot is bouncing the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen, picking at my sleeves, shifting around in the chair. People will actually ask me to stop because I’m moving so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself acting on impulse in situations outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll cut in on people without really meaning to. Like if coworkers are talking, I’ll jump into the conversation, or if the lead is asking something, I’ll answer before they’re even done talking. Then people get irritated with me, and I realize I should’ve waited.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to what you're doing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like with inventory logs or reading the CNC training stuff, I’ll start off trying to focus, but after a few minutes the numbers or words kind of run together. Then I realize I didn’t really take it in, so I have to keep going back and rereading it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your impulsive behaviors are affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I cut people off without meaning to, like I’ll jump into a conversation or answer before someone’s even finished asking. Then they get annoyed with me, and I feel bad because I didn’t mean to be rude.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these impulsive and inattentive behaviors for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. Even back in elementary school I was like that. I had a hard time staying in my seat, I’d talk over the teacher, blurt things out, or just get up and wander around the room. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep leaving my station at work when I’m supposed to stay there. My supervisor said it’s starting to be a safety problem, so… that’s what made me come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're acting on impulse when you leave your station at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it feels impulsive. I don’t really plan to leave—I just kind of pop up or take off from my station before I even think it through. If I’m in a safety meeting or one of those certification classes, my foot is bouncing the whole time, or I’m clicking a pen, picking at my sleeves, shifting around in the chair. People will actually ask me to stop because I’m moving so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself acting on impulse in situations outside of work as well?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll cut in on people without really meaning to. Like if coworkers are talking, I’ll jump into the conversation, or if the lead is asking something, I’ll answer before they’re even done talking. Then people get irritated with me, and I realize I should’ve waited.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble paying attention to what you're doing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Like with inventory logs or reading the CNC training stuff, I’ll start off trying to focus, but after a few minutes the numbers or words kind of run together. Then I realize I didn’t really take it in, so I have to keep going back and rereading it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your impulsive behaviors are affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. I cut people off without meaning to, like I’ll jump into a conversation or answer before someone’s even finished asking. Then they get annoyed with me, and I feel bad because I didn’t mean to be rude.\n\nDoctor: Have you had these impulsive and inattentive behaviors for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty much. Even back in elementary school I was like that. I had a hard time staying in my seat, I’d talk over the teacher, blurt things out, or just get up and wander around the room. I remember getting notes sent home for that kind of stuff.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think I was. In elementary school I was always the kid who couldn’t stay in my seat. I’d talk over the teacher, blurt stuff out, or get up and wander around the room, and I got notes sent home for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your impulsive and inattentive behaviors are causing problems for you in your daily life beyond just the issues at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. It’s not just work in the moment, it kind of follows me. I’ve been written up for safety and production issues, and now I’m worried it could mess up my job and the certification stuff I’m trying to do. And coworkers don’t really want to partner with me during handoffs as much, so it makes the day harder and kind of embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these impulsive and inattentive behaviors consistently throughout your life, without any significant periods where they weren't a problem?\n\nPatient: It’s been there in some way for a long time, but it’s been causing serious problems for about a year and two months now. That’s around when I started trying to tighten up my habits and move toward getting forklift and CNC certified. Since then it’s been harder to brush off, because it’s actually getting in the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your impulsive or inattentive behaviors were so bad that they significantly interfered with your ability to function in daily life or caused a lot of distress for you?\n\nPatient: Yes. At work especially, it’s gotten bad enough that I’m honestly worried about it. I’ve been written up for safety and production problems, and it feels like it could put my job and the certifications I’m trying to get at risk. Even coworkers don’t really want to partner with me during handoffs as much now, so it’s stressful and embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your problems with paying attention or your impulsive behaviors cause you more trouble in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I think the impulsive part causes me more trouble. Like, what really pushed me to come in is I keep leaving my station or popping up during meetings when I’m supposed to stay put. I just kind of take off before I even think about it, and my supervisor is saying it’s becoming a safety problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your impulsive behaviors or trouble paying attention are causing you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does. I get really stressed about it because it’s putting my job at risk. I’ve already been written up for safety and production stuff, and now some coworkers don’t really want to work with me during handoffs. It makes me feel embarrassed and worried, especially with the certifications I’m trying to get.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention or with being too active and impulsive?\n\nPatient: More with being too active and impulsive, I think. Like I’ve said, I keep leaving my station or popping up in meetings when I’m supposed to stay put. It’s like I move before I really think it through, and my supervisor says it’s turning into a safety problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble paying attention or your impulsive behaviors are the bigger problem for you right now?\n\nPatient: The impulsive stuff is the bigger problem right now. I keep leaving my station or getting up in meetings when I’m supposed to stay put, and it’s like I do it before I even think. My supervisor said it’s becoming a safety issue, so that’s really what brought me in.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your trouble with paying attention is as significant a problem for you as your impulsive behaviors?\n\nPatient: It is a problem, yeah. I don’t really stop and think first, I just take off, and my supervisor said it’s becoming a safety problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your attention problems or your hyperactive and impulsive behaviors have been causing you more trouble overall?\n\nPatient: The hyperactive and impulsive stuff is causing me more trouble overall.
